Alexandre Ghiti

3 items
Patches Submitted (3)
[PATCH v4 0/3] mm: fix workingset refaults in the zswap writeback path 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] mm: swap: move LRU insertion out of the swap cache allocator
  • [2/3] mm: swap: refault on swap-in, not in the swap cache allocator
  • [3/3] mm: zswap: preserve the workingset shadow across writeback
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes two issues in the zswap writeback path that cause incorrect workingset refaults. The first issue is over-counting at writeback, where a buffer folio allocated by the shrinker is counted as a refault. The second issue is lost eviction cookies at reclaim, where the original cookie is overwritten when adding the buffer to the swap cache. To fix these issues, the patch preserves the shadow within zswap itself and moves the refault evaluation out of the swap-cache allocator into the swap-in callers. This results in a significant reduction (-48% to -59%) in workingset refaults, activations, and restores for anonymous pages.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-08-25 Last comment 2026-09-08 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v4 0/3] mm: zswap: free cold writeback folios promptly 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] mm: swap: move LRU insertion out of the swap cache allocator Neutral Submitted 2026-08-25Last comment 2026-09-08 Kunwu Chan Nhat Pham
  • [2/3] mm: swap: drop dropbehind swap cache folios on writeback completion Neutral Submitted 2026-08-25Last comment 2026-09-08 Kunwu Chan Nhat Pham
  • [3/3] mm: zswap: drop cold writeback folios via swap dropbehind Neutral Submitted 2026-08-25Last comment 2026-09-08 Yosry Ahmed Alexandre Ghiti Kunwu Chan
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series improves the performance of zswap by promptly freeing cold writeback folios instead of leaving them on the LRU for page reclaim to find and free later. The existing dropbehind mechanism is extended to swap cache folios, allowing zswap to opt into it. This change reduces memory residency and waste in reclaim scans. Patch 1 moves LRU insertion out of the swap cache allocator, patch 2 drops dropbehind swap cache folios on writeback completion, and patch 3 enables zswap to allocate its writeback folio off the LRU and mark it as dropbehind.

[PATCH v5 0/3] mm: zswap: free cold writeback folios promptly 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] mm: swap: move LRU insertion out of the swap cache allocator
  • [2/3] mm: swap: drop dropbehind swap cache folios on writeback completion
  • [3/3] mm: zswap: drop cold writeback folios via swap dropbehind
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series optimizes zswap by promptly freeing cold writeback folios instead of leaving them for page reclaim to find and free later, which wastes a reclaim scan and keeps cold memory resident longer than necessary. The existing dropbehind mechanism is extended to swap cache folios, and zswap is modified to opt into this mechanism. Patch 1 moves LRU insertion out of the swap cache allocator, patch 2 drops dropbehind swap cache folios on writeback completion, and patch 3 allocates the writeback folio off the LRU and marks it dropbehind.

Boris Burkov

8 items
Patches Submitted (5)
[PATCH] btrfs: downgrade the reflink source inode lock Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch downgrades the lock on the source inode during reflink operations from exclusive to shared, reducing latency for concurrent readers. The change also adds a new flag to indicate when an inode is being used as a reflink source, which allows dio writes to take the VFS inode lock in shared mode instead of exclusive. This improves performance by avoiding unnecessary blocking of other shared lock users of the src file.

Last comment 2026-09-11 [PATCH v2 0/3] reduce reflink src inode lock contention Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch reduces contention on inode locks during reflink operations by attempting to perform some writeback outside of the lock and downgrading the src lock to shared when safe. This is achieved through three separate patches that modify Btrfs's reflink handling, including pre-flushing the source before taking locks, skipping flush if the inode has writers, and downgrading the src lock for tree walking.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Filipe Manana Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Filipe Manana questioned the purpose of the variable orig_end, pointing out that it is not used elsewhere in the code and requested its removal. Reviewer Filipe Manana questioned the necessity of introducing a new function, btrfs_wait_for_ordered_extents(), specifically for reflink operations, pointing out that existing function btrfs_wait_ordered_range() already serves the same purpose and can take a range as an argument, which would improve performance for small-range reflinks. Reviewer Filipe Manana noted that the current implementation of reflink file flushing adds unnecessary overhead for small range operations and causes extra IO, and suggested using btrfs_fdatawrite_range() which performs a double flush in case of compression to wait for writeback to start.
Show original comment 
What's the point of orig_end if it's not used elsehwere?
Just this:

u64 end = i_size_read(&inode->vfs_inode);

---

Why do we need another function to wait for ordered extents just for reflinks?

We have btrfs_wait_ordered_range() that does exactly the same...

Besides that, this waits for all ordered extents. If a reflink
operates on a small range, we end up waiting for any ordered extents,
which slows down such ranged reflinks.
btrfs_wait_ordered_range() allows to pass a range.

---

So again, this flushes the entire file, which adds overhead for
reflinks operating on a small range and causes unnecessary IO.

Further, using filemap_flush() is not enough in case we have compression.
The flush call only starts the compression work in an async worker, we
need a second flush call to wait for the compression to finish and for
writeback to start (creating ordered extents).
That's why we have btrfs_fdatawrite_range(), which does the double
flush in case we have compression, and also supports specifying a
range.

Thanks.

Boris Burkov (author) ↳ replying to Filipe Manana Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledges that their initial approach to reducing reflink src inode lock contention was not effective, agrees that btrfs_wait_ordered_range() is a better solution, but expresses difficulty in implementing it Author acknowledged that the contention issue he was trying to address is mitigated by folio locking, but still plans to revert to a previous approach in v3 due to lack of objective benefits.
Show original comment 
TL;DR you are right

I started with btrfs_wait_ordered_range() then got turned around while
exploring the space and ended here which turns out to not be any better.
Thanks for pushing back.

Basically I was battling against cases related to the one helped by the
second patch (concurrent writers producing dirtying which causes write
amplification with the duplicate flush outside the locks) and tried to
create a "lightest weight good enough" flush for this application.

However, I don't think I succeeded and can't think of anything actually
better than btrfs_wait_ordered_range(). (and it's worse for using
SYNC_NONE and doing the whole file...)

---

Interestingly, this turns out to not really matter due to how the folio
locking works today (and that we don't care about 100% perfect
submission anyway). Basically the second folio gets blocked right away
in filemap_flush and waits until the first folio's async compression
finishes and unlocks it. This is the same pattern that Qu is contending
with in his parent-child OE redesign to try to make compressed writeback
look "normal".

There is 0 benefit I can demonstrate objectively from relying on this so
I am going to switch back to plain btrfs_wait_ordered_range() for v3.
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledges a fix is needed, expresses difficulty with implementation, acknowledged a technical detail
Last comment 2026-09-11 [PATCH v2 1/3] btrfs: pre-flush reflink source before taking inode locks Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch optimizes the reflink operation in Btrfs by pre-flushing the source file's dirty pages before taking inode locks, reducing contention between writers and readers. This is done through a single backwards pass that waits for ordered extents on the inode without locking the ordered tree. The approach taken avoids consistency errors associated with concurrent writes and allows for a common case optimization without significant infrastructure investment.

[PATCH v2 2/3] btrfs: skip unlocked reflink source flush if the inode has writers Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the btrfs reflink code to skip optimistic unlocked flushing if there are active writers dirtying pages, as it can result in doubly writing back pages that get dirtied again before a locked flush. The change is advisory and does not guarantee against extra writeback, but helps in simple cases with long open file descriptors or mappings.

[PATCH v2 3/3] btrfs: downgrade the reflink source inode lock for tree walking Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the behavior of reflink in Btrfs to downgrade the lock on the source inode from exclusive to shared during tree walking, reducing contention between reflinks and other file operations. The patch addresses two issues related to direct I/O: it adds a flag to prevent direct I/O writes from taking the lock shared when the source inode is being used as a reflink source, and it ensures that direct I/O reads do not block reflinks by remaining exclusive until after finishing with inode_dio_wait().

Gregory Price

35 items
Patches Submitted (10)
Ongoing Submitted 2026-09-02 Last comment 2026-09-09 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/5] KVM: guest_memfd: bind backing memory to a NUMA node 5 patches
Show 5 individual patches
  • [1/5] mm/mempolicy: add mempolicy_create() Submitted 2026-09-02
  • [2/5] mm/mempolicy: add mpol_set_shared_policy_range() Submitted 2026-09-02
  • [3/5] KVM: guest_memfd: bind backing memory to a NUMA node at creation Submitted 2026-09-02
  • [4/5] selftests: KVM: guest_memfd: let the gmem_test() harness bind a node Submitted 2026-09-02
  • [5/5] selftests: KVM: guest_memfd: test GUEST_MEMFD_FLAG_BIND_NODE Submitted 2026-09-02
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series adds a new flag to the KVM guest memory file descriptor (memfd) API, allowing users to bind the backing memory of the guest to a specific NUMA node. This is done by creating an MPOL_BIND policy for the requested node and installing it over the whole inode, ensuring that every folio is allocated on the specified node without requiring user-space mbind(). The patch series also includes self-test harness support and testing to validate the new functionality. The approach taken is to simplify the guest NUMA topology by binding a single fd to a host node, rather than interleaving it across multiple nodes, which would defeat placement decisions made by the guest.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Ackerley Tng ↳ replying to Gregory Price Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Ackerley Tng suggested alternative approaches to ensure guest_memfd allocations come from a single node, including using cgroups/cpuset, process-level NUMA policy, requesting mmap-ability for memory policy setting, fbind() API, or passing an fd representing a mount with a specified memory policy.
Show original comment 
Instead of a custom API to ensure all guest_memfd allocations come from
a single node, how about these options?

1. Using cgroups/cpuset to constrain allocations (could be troublesome
   if the guest memory is not preallocated, unless the vCPU threads are
   running with the cpuset config)

2. Process-level NUMA policy

3. Why not request the guest_memfd to be mmap-able just to be able to
   set a memory policy?

4. How about something like fbind() that takes an fd and offset range
   instead of mbind(), which has a prerequisite on mmap()?

This doesn't exist yet, but I'm hoping to discuss this at LPC 2026:

5. What if you could pass an fd representing a mount to guest_memfd at
   creation time, so to make all the allocations come from a single node

    Step 1: Create a tmpfs mount, specify mpol for mount to MPOL_BIND
    Step 2: Get some fd representing the tmpfs mount, hand that to
            guest_memfd at creation time
    Step 3: guest_memfd allocations will always come from that tmpfs
            mount, and abide by that tmpfs mount's memory policy.

May I know more about the use case behind this new feature?

Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Ackerley Tng Needs Work LLM
Author clarified that the intent of GUEST_MEMFD_FLAG_BIND_NODE is to bind guest memory to a specific target node, not all system memory for a given process, and acknowledged that the desired node may not have eligible memory. Author acknowledged that requiring a mapping to set node placement defeats the purpose of confidential, host-unmapped guests and plans to enable this for such use cases. The author is addressing concerns about the scope and implementation of guest_memfd, specifically where the policy lives for random file descriptors (fd's) and how a reclaimed inode's policy is handled. The author acknowledges these limitations but prefers to start with a narrow use-case, considering it less over-engineering than implementing per-file mempolicies. Author acknowledges that implementing a single-node binding approach is more feasible than using fbind, but expresses uncertainty about the complexity of details. Author acknowledged that the patch's approach of binding guest memory to a single node is necessary for modeling a confidential VM's topology and explained that this implementation enables the only clear use case.

Show original comment 
We have a similar problem where we wanted only guest memory to be
charged a certain memcg. Currently guest_memfd memory is charge to
mm->owner, which is the thread's parent, so doing the fallocate() in a
thread wasn't good enough. The workaround was to have the fallocate()
done in a separate process (fork) and have the child process's memcg be
set to the desired memcg.

This workaround is kind of awkward, but could it technically work for
NUMA allocations?

---

Off the top of my head the policy would be saved at gi->policy, where
it's stored for mbind() now. fd+offset is just another way of
referencing the offsets to set the policy.

That is a very clunky work-around, I'm not sure it's good to formalize
that kind of design as an expected user behavior.

Whatever the case, the issue of ineligible memory isn't resolved by
this.  A node may be 100% ZONE_MOVABLE, and later entirely unmapped -
literally no eligible memory for kernel allocations for the task itself.

---

It makes sense for anon use cases like this, but for some random file
where fbind() means "put unmapped page cache on node X" the story is
less clear.

---

The only thing i've considered is something like GPU wanting a file
faulted directly onto its node - but that's a mapped region, and that's
handled by existing semantics.

---

Yes, because eventually some kind of overcommit will be desired. May as
well build it in from the start.

Looking forward to LPC :]

~Gregory
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledges a problem, does not propose a solution, acknowledges a limitation
[PATCH] mm/madvise: reclaim isolated folios if PTE restart fails Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch addresses a concurrency issue in the Linux kernel's madvise functionality. When MADV_PAGEOUT is used to collect isolated folios, a concurrent operation can remove or replace the PTE table while the lock is dropped, causing pte_offset_map_lock() to return NULL and bypassing reclaim_pages(). The patch routes this failure through the existing cleanup path, ensuring that any collected folios are reclaimed or put back. This fixes a bug introduced in commit b2f557a21bc8 and resolves a reported issue.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-08-29 Last comment 2026-09-07 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/2] mm/mempolicy: stop copying state in the interleave paths 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm/mempolicy: use SRCU for the weighted interleave state Submitted 2026-08-29
  • [2/2] mm/mempolicy: stop copying the nodemask in the interleave paths Neutral Submitted 2026-08-29Last comment 2026-09-07 Rakie Kim Gregory Price (author)
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch eliminates unnecessary copies of nodemask and node weights in the interleave paths of the Linux kernel's memory policy module. It achieves this by using SRCU to pin node weights in place, operating directly on pol->nodes for nodemasks, and clamping the walk to a single pass. The changes improve performance but may introduce a small trade-off in distribution accuracy during rebind operations.

Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Rakie Kim Needs Work LLM
Author responded to Rakie Kim's feedback, stating that widening the SRCU window to include the cpuset cookie does not fix anything and may even be counterproductive by calculating weight totals unnecessarily.
Show original comment 
I don't think this actually fixes anything?

But basically the proposal is to widen the SRCU() window further to
include the cpuset cookie entirely.

e.g.

SRCU() {
	cpuset_cookie() {
		for_each_node_mask(node, pol->nodes)
			weight_total += ...
			nnodes++;
	}

	/* ... snip - single node quick-exit ... */

        /* ... actual multi-node bulk allocation ... */
	for_each_node_mask(node, pol->nodes) {
		nr_allocated = __alloc_pages_bulk(gfp, node, ...);

		/*
                 * At this point, due to a torn read from pol->nodes
		 * we can visit a node that wasn't present previously
		 * or we can skip a node that was present previously.
		 *
		 * In either case, weight_total is the wrong value for
		 * the set of nodes being walked anyway - we are going
		 * to skew in the distribution no matter what.
		 */
	}
}

I'm not sure widening the SRCU window is worth it here, it doesn't
actually buy us anything.

Also we'd be calculating the weight total every time even when there's a
scenario where we quick-exit because the entire allocation fits in the
first node in the mask.

Rakie Kim ↳ replying to Gregory Price Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
The reviewer pointed out a potential issue with the timing of two reads: one to calculate the total number of nodes and another to read the nodemask, which can lead to inconsistent bounds if the mask changes during the walk. They suggested adding a counter to the sum loop where it already is, but acknowledged that this does not address the torn read case.
Show original comment 
Sorry, I did not explain that well. I was not suggesting moving the
sum into the cookie loop or widening the SRCU section - only adding
the counter to the sum loop where it already is:

	/* calculate total, detect system default usage */
	nnodes = 0;
	for_each_node_mask(node, pol->nodes) {
		weight_total += table ? table[node] : 1;
		nnodes++;
	}

The order of the function does not change, so the quick-exit path
still returns before this loop runs - the sum is not computed in
that case either way.

What I had in mind is the gap between the two reads. Say the policy
starts with two nodes, every weight is 10, and 100 pages are
requested:

	/* the mask is {0,1} here */
	do {
		cpuset_mems_cookie = read_mems_allowed_begin();
		nnodes = nodes_weight(pol->nodes);	/* nnodes = 2 */
	} while (read_mems_allowed_retry(cpuset_mems_cookie));

	/* a rebind grows the mask to {0,1,2,3} at this point */

	/* calculate total, detect system default usage */
	for_each_node_mask(node, pol->nodes)
		weight_total += ...;			/* 10 * 4 = 40 */

	rounds = rem_pages / weight_total;		/* 100 / 40 = 2 */

	for (i = 0; i < nnodes; i++)			/* bounded by 2 */
		...

The distribution is planned from a total of 40, which spreads the
100 pages over four nodes, but the walk is still bounded by the two
nodes counted earlier. It places 60 pages and returns, and the
caller allocates the remaining 40 one page at a time. The mask does
not have to change again during the walk for this to happen.

You are right that a torn read during the walk still skews the
distribution, and this does not change that. It only removes the
case where the bound and the total start out inconsistent. If that
is not worth the extra line, I am fine either way.

Thanks for looking at it.

Rakie Kim
View on lore ↗
Signals: potential issue with timing of two reads, inconsistent bounds
Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Rakie Kim Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged that the patch has a limitation in handling concurrent rebinding of node masks, which can lead to incorrect weight totals and division by zero errors. He chose not to fix this issue, opting for a simpler solution that ensures safety rather than accuracy.
Show original comment 
Consider:

	/* nodemask: {0,1} */
 	for_each_node_mask(node, pol->nodes) {
 		weight_total += ...;
		nnodes++;
	}
 
 	/* a rebind grows the mask to {2,3,4,5} */
 
 	rounds = rem_pages / weight_total;		/* 100 / 10 = 10 */
 	for (i = 0; i < nnodes; i++)			/* bounded by 2 */
 		...

in this scenario every value is wrong.  The weight total was calculated
based on {0,1} and the loop will use {2,3} weights and ignore {4,5}
entirely.

It's the nature of the mechanism and race - best we can do is ensure
safety.  Ensuring correct distributions would likely require locks or
reworking the entire weight mechanism.

I'd rather keep the change simple (cookie the value that can cause a
div/0) and leave the math alone.

~Gregory

[PATCH v2 0/2] mm: refactor zonelist constructors and iterators 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm: refactor find_next_best_node to find_next_best_node_in
  • [2/2] mm/page_alloc: refactor build_node_zonelist() out of build_zonelists()
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch refactors the zonelist constructors and iterators in the Linux kernel to make them more flexible and modular. The existing behavior of picking nodes from N_MEMORY is made explicit, and a new function find_next_best_node_in() allows callers to specify the candidate set. Additionally, the per-node construction loop is extracted into a separate function build_node_zonelist(), which can be used with different candidate nodemasks and destination zonelists. These changes enable the creation of private node zonelists and improve the existing interfaces without introducing any functional changes.

Last comment 2026-09-12 [PATCH v2 0/2] mm: stop calling pmd_folio() on special PMDs 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm/mempolicy: use vm_normal_folio_pmd() in queue_folios_pmd() Neutral Last comment 2026-09-12 Zi Yan
  • [2/2] mm/madvise: use vm_normal_folio_pmd() in cold/pageout PMD range Neutral Last comment 2026-09-12 Zi Yan
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses an issue where special PMDs (Page Map Directory entries) are being treated as regular PMDs, leading to incorrect behavior when accessing the folio behind them. The problem arises from two page table walkers that resolve the folio using pmd_folio(), which is only valid for a PMD mapping a refcounted struct page. This patch series modifies these walkers to use vm_normal_folio() instead, ensuring consistency with the PTE halves of both walkers. As a result, mbind(MPOL_MF_STRICT) over a PMD mapped VM_PFNMAP region now returns 0 rather than -EIO.

[PATCH] proc/task_mmu: handle special PMDs in clear_refs and pagemap Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes a bug in the Linux kernel's /proc/PID/pagemap and /proc/PID/clear_refs functionality, which causes a kernel oops when handling special PMDs (Page Map Directory Entries) that are not backed by a struct page. The fix uses vm_normal_folio_pmd() and vm_normal_page_pmd() to handle these special cases, matching the behavior of PTE paths. This patch has been tested on both broken and fixed kernels using QEMU with a test driver.

[PATCH 0/2] rtc: cmos: Fix ACPI RTC alarm handling 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] rtc: cmos: Disable ACPI RTC event before handling it
  • [2/2] rtc: cmos: Honor explicit use_acpi_alarm parameter value
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch addresses an issue where the ACPI RTC alarm handling in the Linux kernel's cmos driver can stop delivering events prematurely due to a race condition between the SCI handler and dispatch queues. The fix involves moving the clear and disable operations before dispatching the RTC interrupt, ensuring that rearming of alarms always wins the race. Additionally, the patch honors an explicit parameter value for using ACPI alarm handling, preventing automatic platform selection from overriding it.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-09-04 Last comment 2026-09-07 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/2] sched/numa: stop VMA scan filters from gating promotion 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] sched/numa: do not let the per-VMA PID filter gate promotion Neutral Submitted 2026-09-04Last comment 2026-09-04 Gregory Price
  • [2/2] sched/numa: scan read-only file mappings in tiering mode Neutral Submitted 2026-09-04Last comment 2026-09-04 Gregory Price
Neutral LLM

This patch series aims to stop VMA scan filters from gating promotion in the Linux kernel's scheduler and NUMA (Non-Uniform Memory Access) subsystem. The current implementation applies a series of VMA-level filters before marking a VMA for hint faults, which can lead to issues with memory tiering. The patches modify the per-VMA PID filter and read-only file mapping exclusion to allow promotion candidates to be added without removing existing filters.

Last comment 2026-09-11 [PATCH v2 0/4] sched/numa: stop VMA scan filters from gating promotion 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] mm: support promotion-only NUMA hinting scans
  • [2/4] mm: allow shared folios to be promoted to a fast tier
  • [3/4] sched/numa: scan read-only file mappings in tiering mode
  • [4/4] sched/numa: do not let VMA PID activity gate promotion
Neutral LLM

This patch series aims to improve NUMA balancing by separating promotion-only scans from socket-placement scans, allowing eligible shared folios to be promoted and enabling read-only file mappings and PID-inactive VMAs to be scanned for promotion without re-enabling placement sampling. The changes involve adding a new protection walk flag, MM_CP_PROT_NUMA_PROMO_ONLY, and modifying the existing code to track the last placement scan separately.

Conversion of process/thread iterators to for_each_*_rculock, raising questions about the use of cleanup.h and goto statements.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants

Gregory Price Needs Work LLM
Raised concerns about mixing cleanup.h and goto statements in the patch, citing potential confusion and bugs. Suggested re-examining the use of for_each_*_rculock with cleanup.h.
Show original comment 
On Mon, Sep 07, 2026 at 11:54:41PM +0200, Oleg Nesterov wrote:
> >
> > This might be my - admittedly shallow - understanding of scoped_guard,
> > but does scoped guard handle goto correctly?
> 
> Yes, it does,
> 
> > and why it's generally discouraged to mix cleanup.h and goto.
> 
> Hmm, why? I didn't know...
> 

one of the reasons to use cleanup.h it is to eliminate the "goto
for exit cleanup" mechanism in the first place (says so in the first
paragraph of cleanup.h).

Later in the header is explicit says don't mix:
 
 * Lastly, given that the benefit of cleanup helpers is removal of
 * "goto", and that the "goto" statement can jump between scopes, the
 * expectation is that usage of "goto" and cleanup helpers is never
 * mixed in the same function. I.e. for a given routine, convert all
 * resources that need a "goto" cleanup to scope-based cleanup, or
 * convert none of them.

This particular patch only does a partial conversion - so it's a
bit of an anti-pattern.

Reading up on scoped_guard a bit more it seems you're right it handles
the issue to jump-out, but i can see why cleanup.h says "don't do that"
because it's confusing to look at / hard to reason about.

~Gregory

Last comment 2026-09-09 Re: [PATCH 0/5] KVM: guest_memfd: bind backing memory to a NUMA node Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series aims to bind guest memory in KVM to a specific NUMA node, rather than all system memory for a process. The goal is to enable confidential and isolated virtual machines that are quarantined to a particular device without affecting the host's memory. To achieve this, the patch reduces the scope of the problem by focusing on the narrowest use-case, which is binding guest memory to a NUMA node. This approach is considered more feasible than other alternatives, such as implementing per-file memory policies or using fbind().

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Ackerley Tng ↳ replying to Gregory Price Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Ackerley Tng noted that a similar problem was encountered where guest memory was charged to the thread's parent mm->owner, and suggested using a workaround involving a separate process to set the child process's memcg to the desired one, which could potentially work for NUMA allocations. Reviewer Ackerley Tng questioned the scope and feasibility of implementing per-file memory policies for guest_memfd, suggesting that a default filesystem-wide mempolicy might be more suitable and easier to implement. Reviewer noted that the current approach of requiring a mapping to get node-placement is defeating the point of enabling confidential, host-unmapped guest memory isolation and suggested exploring an alternative approach similar to HugeTLBfs. Reviewer Ackerley Tng questioned whether lazy allocation is necessary, suggesting that pre-allocating memory using fallocate() before the guest accesses pages might be a viable alternative.
Show original comment 
We have a similar problem where we wanted only guest memory to be
charged a certain memcg. Currently guest_memfd memory is charge to
mm->owner, which is the thread's parent, so doing the fallocate() in a
thread wasn't good enough. The workaround was to have the fallocate()
done in a separate process (fork) and have the child process's memcg be
set to the desired memcg.

This workaround is kind of awkward, but could it technically work for
NUMA allocations?

---

Off the top of my head the policy would be saved at gi->policy, where
it's stored for mbind() now. fd+offset is just another way of
referencing the offsets to set the policy.

---

Great! :) Cya!

Along the above lines, for HugeTLBfs we'd do something similar, and the
mount's fd would also indicate the HugeTLB page size.

Relating to memcg above, this doesn't really help though, since tmpfs
doesn't offer a way to configure memcgs.

---

Is lazy allocation a requirement for you? (as opposed to fallocate()-ing
before the guest touches pages)
View on lore ↗

Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Ackerley Tng Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged that the proposed work-around is clunky and not a good user behavior expectation, but did not agree to fix it; also pointed out that the issue of ineligible memory remains unresolved. Author acknowledged that the scope of the patch is not suitable for all system memory, but expressed uncertainty about how to handle random file descriptors and reclaimed inodes. Author acknowledged Ackerley Tng's concern about the scope of the patch and the need for per-file memory policies, but expressed skepticism about implementing such a feature, suggesting it might be over-engineering. Author acknowledged that future overcommit handling is a consideration and decided to include it in the initial implementation.
Show original comment 
That is a very clunky work-around, I'm not sure it's good to formalize
that kind of design as an expected user behavior.

Whatever the case, the issue of ineligible memory isn't resolved by
this.  A node may be 100% ZONE_MOVABLE, and later entirely unmapped -
literally no eligible memory for kernel allocations for the task itself.

---

It makes sense for anon use cases like this, but for some random file
where fbind() means "put unmapped page cache on node X" the story is
less clear.

---

The only thing i've considered is something like GPU wanting a file
faulted directly onto its node - but that's a mapped region, and that's
handled by existing semantics.

---

Yes, because eventually some kind of overcommit will be desired. May as
well build it in from the start.

Looking forward to LPC :]

Show original comment 
On Thu, Sep 10, 2026 at 04:05:36PM +0200, David Hildenbrand (Arm) wrote:
> On 9/10/26 15:40, Gregory Price wrote:
> > On Thu, Sep 10, 2026 at 01:30:04PM +0200, David Hildenbrand (Arm) wrote:
> >> On 9/2/26 21:46, Gregory Price wrote:
> >>> guest_memfd allocates its folios through a per-inode shared mempolicy.
> >>>
> >>> Today that policy can only be set after the fact, with mbind() on a host
> >>> mmap of the fd. That requires the fd to be mappable, and it cannot reach
> >>> folios that are only ever guest-faulted. Neither holds for a
> >>> non-mappable (confidential) guest_memfd.
> >>
> >> That looks like an investment into a mechanism we will soon consider legacy, so
> >> I am not convinced this is the right approach?
> >>
> > 
> > Hm, I think i've missed recent work in this space / this context.
> > Any suggested reading here? Don't want to eat too much of your time.
> 
> So the big development happening right now is in-place conversion (having shared
> and private pages managed in guest_memfd):
> 
> https://lore.kernel.org/r/20260830-gmem-inplace-conversion-v12-0-85e5fd25252a@google.com
> 
> 
> The final series where we added mempolicy support should be:
> 
> https://lore.kernel.org/linux-mm/20250827175247.83322-2-shivankg@amd.com/

> guest_memfd.

Ah yes, i do remember this series.  In fact...

https://lore.kernel.org/linux-mm/aPD-dbl5KWNSHu5R@gourry-fedora-PF4VCD3F/

```
So this inode mempolicy in guest_memfd is really acting more as a the
filesystem-default mempolicy, which you want to survive even if userland
never maps the memory/unmaps the memory.

So the relationship is more like

guest_memfd -> creates fd/inode <- copies task mempolicy (if set)
  vm:  allocates memory via filemap_get_folio_mpol()
  userland mmap(fd):
  	creates new inode<->vma mapping
	vma->mpol = kvm_gmem_get_policy()
	calls to set/get_policy/mbind go through kvm_gmem
```

And quick skim of in-place conversion (wow that's dense) 

```
  #define KVM_MEMORY_ATTRIBUTE_PRIVATE           (1ULL << 3)

  Set attributes for a range of offsets within a guest_memfd to
  KVM_MEMORY_ATTRIBUTE_PRIVATE to limit the specified guest_memfd backed
  memory range for guest use. Even if KVM_CAP_GUEST_MEMFD_MMAP is
  supported, after a successful call to set KVM_MEMORY_ATTRIBUTE_PRIVATE,
  the requested range will not be mappable into host userspace and will
  only be mappable by the guest.
```

I guess the thought here would be something like...

1. create guest memfd
2. mmap(fd)               -> prior to full guest setup
3. mbind(buf)             -> shared policy hits the inode
4. munmap(buf)            -> policy is NOT reaped
5. set ATTRIBUTE_PRIVATE
6. no longer user mappable

That doesn't seem unreasonable

~Gregory

Provided context and background information on recent work in guest_memfd, including the introduction of in-place conversion. Suggested that the patch author consider these developments when revising their patch series.

Last comment 2026-09-12 Re: [PATCH v2 0/2] mm: stop calling pmd_folio() on special PMDs Neutral LLM

The patch addresses a bug in the mm/mempolicy code where a VM_PFNMAP region's huge_fault installs a PMD through vmf_insert_pfn_pmd(), causing a folio to land in unpopulated vmemmap. The fix uses vm_normal_folio_pmd() instead of pmd_folio() and adds a NULL check to prevent the bad folio from carrying on into migrate_folio_add() and folio_isolate_lru().

 		qword_addint(bp, blen, p->info->number);

+static int can_reexport_via_fsidnum(struct exportent *exp, struct statfs *st)
+{
+	if (st->f_type != 0x6969 /* NFS_SUPER_MAGIC */)
+		return 0;
+
+	return exp->e_reexport == REEXP_PREDEFINED_FSIDNUM ||
+	       exp->e_reexport == REEXP_AUTO_FSIDNUM;
+}
+
+/* What to hand the kernel for one (path, export) pair */
+struct export_attrs {
+	int		flags;
+	uint32_t	fsidnum;
+	int		sec_mask;	/* mask for the per-flavor flags */
+	int		sec_extra;	/* extra per-flavor flags */
+	char		uuid[16];
+	bool		have_uuid;
+};
+
+/*
+ * An upcall path may be a submount below the exported one, when the export
+ * is marked crossmnt.  Such a submount is a filesystem in its own right, so
+ * it must not inherit the parent's fsid= or uuid= - if it does, both end up
+ * claiming the same filehandles and the client sees ESTALE.
+ *
+ * Returns 0, or -1 with errno set if @path cannot be exported at all.
+ */
+static int export_attrs_build(struct export_attrs *ea, char *path,
+			      struct exportent *exp)
+{
+	int different_fs = strcmp(path, exp->e_path) != 0;
+	int flag_mask = different_fs ? ~NFSEXP_FSID : ~0;
+	int do_fsidnum = 0;
+
+	memset(ea, 0, sizeof(*ea));
+	ea->fsidnum = exp->e_fsid;
+
+	if (different_fs) {
+		struct statfs st;
+
+		if (nfsd_path_statfs(path, &st)) {
+			xlog(L_WARNING, "unable to statfs %s", path);
+			errno = EINVAL;
+			return -1;
+		}
+
+		/* A re-exported submount gets an fsid= of its own instead */
+		if (can_reexport_via_fsidnum(exp, &st)) {
+			do_fsidnum = 1;
+			flag_mask = ~0;
+		}
+	}
+
+	if (do_fsidnum) {
+		uint32_t search_fsidnum = 0;
+
+		if (exp->e_reexport != REEXP_NONE &&
+		    reexpdb_fsidnum_by_path(path, &search_fsidnum,
+			    exp->e_reexport == REEXP_AUTO_FSIDNUM) == 0) {
+			errno = EINVAL;
+			return -1;
+		}
+		ea->fsidnum = search_fsidnum;
+		ea->flags = exp->e_flags | NFSEXP_FSID;
+		ea->sec_extra = NFSEXP_FSID;
+	} else {
+		ea->flags = exp->e_flags & flag_mask;
+	}
+	ea->sec_mask = flag_mask;
+
+	if (exp->e_uuid && !different_fs) {
+		get_uuid(exp->e_uuid, 16, ea->uuid);
+		ea->have_uuid = true;
+	} else if ((exp->e_flags & flag_mask & NFSEXP_FSID) == 0) {
+		ea->have_uuid = uuid_by_path(path, 0, 16, ea->uuid);
+	}
+
+	return 0;
+}
+

  *

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series allows userland to handle rpcbind registration for nfsd listeners, providing a new optional flag in the netlink listener set interface that tells the kernel to skip rpcbind registration. This is done by adding a new userspace-rpcbind flag to the listener_set function and honouring it in the listener_set reply. The patch also includes changes to report registerable programs in the listener_set reply and exercises the userspace-rpcbind listener_set flag with selftests. The goal is to allow userland to handle rpcbind registration, while still keeping kernel handling of NLM registration intact.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Chuck Lever Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that the patch's documentation incorrectly states that listeners with an rpcbind netid are omitted from the reply, when in fact RDMA listeners have netids but were not registered by NFSD and thus are not a regression. The reviewer, Chuck Lever, noted that the patch's listener_set reply is conditional on the userspace-rpcbind flag, which is not supported by the ynl (netlink) specification. He suggested either always sending a reply or removing the registration data from the listener-set and placing it in a separate get operation.
Show original comment 
"listeners that have an rpcbind netid" overstates the set. RDMA
listeners have netids (rdma, rdma6) but are omitted from the
reply.

Following the way Solaris handles RDMA transport, NFSD never
registered RDMA itself, so that is not a regression, but the doc
should say what your filter actually selects.

---

The spec declares an unconditional reply, but patch 4 sends one only
when the request carried userspace-rpcbind. ynl has no way to express
a conditional reply, and no other in-tree spec does this.

Either always send the reply (drop the userspace_rpcbind gate in
patch 4, so a plain caller simply ignores it), or leave listener-set
without a reply and put the registration data in a separate get
operation. I lean toward always replying; that's is a little more
"declarative".


-- 
Chuck Lever (Come to NFS bake-a-thon! https://nfsv4bat.org)

Signals: requested correction to documentation, conditional reply, yneL limitation
Chuck Lever Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Chuck Lever noted that the ownership check for rpcbind registration only enforces one direction, and proposed moving the mismatch test into nfsd_create_serv() to make both paths agree, which would require a different extack message but keep the same errno.
Show original comment 
This ownership check lives only in the netlink path, so it is
enforced in one direction. Consider: the serv is created here with
userspace-rpcbind, then an old rpc.nfsd writes a socket fd to
/proc/fs/nfsd/portlist. __write_ports_addfd() calls
nfsd_create_serv(net, false), which returns 0 at the existing
"if (nn->nfsd_serv) return 0;" without looking at the flag.
svc_addsock() then goes through svc_setup_socket() with pmap_register
set, and svc_register() returns 0 early because sv_no_rpcbind is set.
The write succeeds, and the new listener is neither registered by the
kernel nor reported to the userspace owner. The reverse order gets
-EBUSY from the check above.

Moving the mismatch test into nfsd_create_serv(), where every creator
already funnels, would make both paths agree. The extack message would
need a different home, but the errno is the important part.


-- 
Chuck Lever (Come to NFS bake-a-thon! https://nfsv4bat.org)
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Chuck Lever Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Chuck Lever noted that in userland rpcbind mode, if a listener creation fails due to an address already in use error, the kernel will only keep the last created listener and not tear it down, leading to unexpected behavior where nfsd is serving on the original port without being registered in rpcbind. He suggested either tearing down all listeners when any of them fail or sending a reply with the existing listeners whenever at least one exists. Reviewer Chuck Lever noted that genlmsg_new() already adds GENL_HDRLEN via genlmsg_total_size(), causing the provided comment to double-count it and potentially mislead future developers, and suggested passing only the attribute payload. Reviewer Chuck Lever suggested that a small helper function be added to emit one address nest for an xprt, called from both loops in nfsd_nl_listener_get_doit() and the loop in this patch, to keep them synchronized and avoid code drift. reviewer noted that the ACL block in nfsd_support_acl_version() is redundant and can be removed, as it only handles a special case for the ACL program
Show original comment 
The second sentence of the comment is not true after a partial
failure. Take a request with userspace-rpcbind carrying {tcp:2049,
tcp:<port already in use>}. The creation loop continues past the
second failure and keeps only the last errno. The 2049 xprt is on
sv_permsocks, so the serv survives the list_empty() check below, but
this gate skips the reply and the caller sees -EADDRINUSE. nfsd is
now serving on 2049 with nothing registered in rpcbind, and the caller
was never told which listeners came up or which programs to register.
The same end state results when nfsd_nl_listener_set_msg() itself
fails with -ENOMEM.

In kernel-owned mode the survivor would already have been registered
inside svc_xprt_create_from_sa(), so this is a behavioral gap specific
to the new mode. Either tear down the listeners that were created when
any of them fails, or send the reply whenever at least one listener
exists on the serv, regardless of err. The second keeps the existing
partial-success semantics; the caller can then act on what it got.

---

genlmsg_new() already adds GENL_HDRLEN via genlmsg_total_size(), so
this term double-counts it. Harmless, but the comment will mislead the
next person sizing a reply. Pass only the attribute payload here.

---

This is the same nest emission as the loop in
nfsd_nl_listener_get_doit(), apart from the XPT_RPCB_UNREG filter and
the errno (that one returns -EINVAL). A small helper that emits one
addr nest for an xprt, called from both loops, would keep the two from
drifting, with the size accounting above as the third place to keep
in step.

Also, as noted on patch 2, this filter selects TCP and UDP only; the
spec doc should match.

---

The ACL block is redundant with the first test. The ACL program has
pg_nvers = NFSD_ACL_NRVERS and pg_vers = nfsd_acl_version, whose
slots 0 and 1 and any compiled-out version are NULL, and
nfsd_support_acl_version() checks exactly that range and that array.
Dropping the block removes the function's only program-number special
case and the #if with it.


-- 
Chuck Lever (Come to NFS bake-a-thon! https://nfsv4bat.org)
View on lore ↗
Signals: behavioral gap, unexpected behavior, harmless
[PATCH RFC nfs-utils 0/7] nfsdctl: allow it to handle rpcbind registration and deregistration 7 patches
Show 7 individual patches
  • [1/7] nfsdctl: sync nfsd_netlink.h with the kernel
  • [2/7] nfsdctl: probe the policy of any command
  • [3/7] nfsdctl: share the listener nest parser
  • [4/7] nfsdctl: add rpcbind registration helpers
  • [5/7] nfsdctl: register nfsd listeners with rpcbind
  • [6/7] nfsdctl: clear rpcbind entries when the last thread stops
  • [7/7] nfsdctl: add missing break in update_listeners()
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patchset allows the nfsdctl tool to handle rpcbind registration and deregistration, rather than relying on the kernel to do so. This is achieved by adding support for a new kernel option that tells it not to register with rpcbind, and instead calls out to rpcbind using libtirpc client interfaces from userland. The patchset includes changes to nfsdctl to handle this new behavior, including registering listeners with rpcbind and clearing entries when the last thread stops.

Last comment 2026-09-09 Re: [PATCH v6 1/2] fuse: bound io-uring payload copies to the registered buffer size Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a bug in the FUSE (Filesystem in Userspace) driver that breaks zero-copy reads when the payload length is zero. The fix involves removing a BUG_ON statement and returning an error instead, making it easier to backport to stable kernels. Additionally, the patch separates the errno handling for FUSE_SETXATTR into its own patch.

Re: [PATCH] mm: zswap: avoid unnecessary xarray lookup in zswap_store() Reviewed-by Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch refactors the zswap code to avoid unnecessary xarray lookups in the zswap_store function. It introduces a new helper function __zswap_invalidate that takes a type and offset as arguments, allowing for more efficient invalidation of swap entries. The existing zswap_invalidate function is simplified by calling this new helper function. This change improves performance by reducing the number of xarray lookups required.

  • [5/7] mm/page_counter: introduce an asynchronous drainer Neutral Submitted 2026-08-31Last comment 2026-09-07 Shakeel Butt Joshua Hahn
  • [6/7] mm/memcontrol: convert memcg to use page_counter_stock Submitted 2026-08-31
  • [7/7] mm/memcontrol: add stock to the memsw page_counter Neutral Submitted 2026-08-31 Michal Koutn
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series moves the per-cpu 'stock' from mem_cgroup to page_counter, allowing for more efficient and independent stock management. The stock is now a property of each counter rather than the CPU, eliminating the need for cross-memcg interference and random victim eviction. This change also enables transparent charging and refilling of stock through page_counter_try_charge(). As a result, multiple sources of stock management can be added to mem_cgroup without tightly coupling them to struct mem_cgroup.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-08-31 Last comment 2026-09-07 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v2 0/2] Fix deferred_split_isolate() and drop the split workaround 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm/huge_memory: do not touch frozen folios in deferred_split_isolate() Neutral Submitted 2026-08-31Last comment 2026-09-07 Lance Yang Usama Arif Baolin Wang David (Arm)
  • [2/2] mm/huge_memory: dequeue the deferred split after the split freeze Neutral Submitted 2026-08-31Last comment 2026-09-07 Lance Yang David (Arm) Kiryl Shutsemau (author)
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series fixes a bug in the deferred_split_isolate() function, which incorrectly handles frozen folios and leads to wrong results when !folio_try_get() is not caused by folio_put(). The bug was inert in upstream code but was discovered during review of another series. The patches fix the issue by removing the workaround for this behavior from __folio_freeze_and_split_unmapped(), and add fixes and closes tags. The changes were tested in a VM with various tests, including split_huge_page_test, folio_split_race_test, and cow.

Last comment 2026-09-07 [PATCH v5 0/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Make the queue depths tunable, and shrink them in a kdump kernel 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Add a cmdq_max_entries module parameter Neutral Last comment 2026-09-07 Nicolin Chen
  • [2/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Default queue depths to one page in a kdump kernel Neutral Last comment 2026-09-07 Nicolin Chen
Neutral LLM

This patch series introduces a new module parameter, cmdq_max_entries, to make the queue depths tunable in the iommu/arm-smmu-v3 driver. The default queue depth is set to one page in a kdump kernel, but an explicit value for cmdq_max_entries can override this default. The patches also include some refactoring and renaming of variables to improve code clarity.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Nicolin Chen Reviewed-by Tested-by Positive LLM
Approved the patches with some minor suggestions for improvement. Noted a potential type mismatch issue with ilog2() and suggested using max_t(u32, ilog2(entries), floor) instead.
Show original comment 
On Mon, Sep 07, 2026 at 10:58:34AM +0100, Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) wrote:

I still think that cmdq_max_n_shift can slightly tidy things here.

> +static u32 arm_smmu_queue_max_n_shift(u32 ceiling, u32 ent_sz_shift,
> +				      u32 entries)

Here, all three inputs would have been "shifts", instead of two
"shifts" and one "number of entries".

> +{
> +	u32 floor = PAGE_SHIFT - ent_sz_shift;
> +
> +	if (!entries)
> +		return ceiling;
> +
> +	return min(ceiling, max(ilog2(entries), floor));

And I see Sashiko keeps complaining against the ilog2 here:
"
Does this trigger a build failure due to strict type checking?
The ilog2(entries) expression returns a signed int when entries is derived
from a runtime value like the module parameter, while floor is explicitly
declared as an unsigned u32.
Since the kernel's max() macro enforces strict type compatibility, this
mismatch evaluates to 0 in the type checking logic, triggering a signedness
error during compilation.
Should this use max_t(u32, ilog2(entries), floor) instead?
"

Though Sashiko complained about the min_t() in v4 as well..

With cmdq_max_n_shift, there is no ilog2 and no type mismatch.

If you can address or justify the Sashiko finding, I am fine with
the cmdq_max_entries though..

So, in either way, the patch looks good to me,

Reviewed-by: Nicolin Chen <nicolinc@nvidia.com>
Tested-by: Nicolin Chen <nicolinc@nvidia.com>
Signals: approval
[PATCH v6 0/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Make the queue depths tunable, and shrink them in a kdump kernel 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Add a cmdq_max_n_shift module parameter
  • [2/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Default queue depths to one page in a kdump kernel
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch set makes the queue depths in the ARM SMMU v3 driver tunable, allowing users to adjust them based on their system's needs. The default queue depth is also changed for kdump kernels, where it is reduced to one page to conserve memory. This change was motivated by the high memory usage of the queues, which can be a problem in systems with limited resources. The patch introduces a new module parameter called cmdq_max_n_shift that allows users to set the maximum queue depth as a power of 2. The patch has been tested on various configurations and has shown significant reductions in memory usage without any performance impact.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-09-04 Last comment 2026-09-07 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 00/12] mm/collapse: separate a collapse from its callers Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch set separates the collapse engine from its callers, introducing a clear interface and policy between them. The collapse engine is moved to khugepaged.c, but it now has an interface that allows callers to specify their own policies and interact with the engine without reaching into each other's code. The patch also moves MADV_COLLAPSE to madvise.c, where it belongs, and introduces four new calls: collapse_control_init, collapse_policy_set, collapse_scan_pmd, and collapse_run_pmd. This separation improves modularity and maintainability of the code.

Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed concerns about locking and counter management in khugepaged.c, agreeing to drop mmgrab() references and rename cc->mthp_present_ptes to eligible_ptes for clarity.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

madvise_collapse() holds an mmgrab() reference across its work. It is
redundant. Every caller already holds mm_users:

 - madvise(2) works on current->mm, which lives as long as the task is in
   the syscall;
 - process_madvise(2) reaches a remote mm through mm_access(), which takes
   an mm_users reference and holds it until the syscall returns;
 - io_uring passes current->mm;
 - DAMON takes one with get_task_mm() and drops it after the call.

Drop the mmgrab()/mmdrop() pair.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

collapse_single_pmd() bumps khugepaged_pages_collapsed for its caller, and
tests cc->is_khugepaged to know whether it should: the counter belongs to
the daemon, and MADV_COLLAPSE must not touch it.

The daemon sees every result of every collapse it asks for, so it can keep
its own counter without the shared path testing who called.

From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

The name says less than the bit means.  A set bit means not only that the
PTE is present, but also that it passed the other checks: uffd,
lazyfree, anonymity, sharing.  The PTE can be considered a collapse
source.

mthp_collapse() then reads the bitmap starting at the PMD order, so the
bitmap is not specific to mTHP either.

Name it for what a set bit means, and update the comments that named it.

No functional change.

2.54.0

From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

khugepaged.c holds both the users of collapse and the machinery that
performs it.  The daemon's scan loop, the sysfs tunables, MADV_COLLAPSE's
entry point and the collapse itself all sit in one file and reach into
each other freely.  Nothing marks where a user ends and the engine
begins.

Start drawing that line.

Add mm/collapse.h for what the two sides have to agree on:

  - enum scan_result - what the engine hands back;
  - struct collapse_control - the state a request carries.

And two constants move with them:

  - KHUGEPAGED_MAX_PTES_LIMIT -> COLLAPSE_MAX_PTES_LIMIT;
  - KHUGEPAGED_MIN_MTHP_ORDER -> COLLAPSE_MIN_MTHP_ORDER.

Neither is a fact about the daemon, so both lose the KHUGEPAGED_ prefix.

No functional change.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+

+

+
+/* The smallest order a collapse will build */
+#define COLLAPSE_MIN_MTHP_ORDER		2
+

+	SCAN_FAIL,

+

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: agreed_to_restructure, clarified_technical_details
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern that tests scattered through the collapse path decide what a collapse is allowed to do by asking whether khugepaged started it, and explained that this should be handled by struct collapse_policy filled by the caller. The author confirmed that khugepaged fills the policy once per scan pass, MADV_COLLAPSE once per call, with no change in behavior for max_ptes_* limits and defrag setting behind allocation mask.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

Tests scattered through the collapse path decide what a collapse is
allowed to do by asking whether khugepaged started it.  Between them they
settle:

 - which VMAs are eligible, and how hard to try for a folio;
 - how many empty, swapped-out or shared PTEs a window may contain, and
   whether a sub-PMD window is held to a stricter rule than a PMD;
 - whether a range has to look used, and whether a MADV_FREE'd page is
   left alone;
 - whether the PMD is mapped as part of the request, and whether dirty
   pages are worth writing back and retrying.

None of those is a fact about khugepaged.  Each is something the caller
decided before asking, and the collapse code should not have to look up
who called to find out.

Add struct collapse_policy for the caller to fill: khugepaged from its
own settings, MADV_COLLAPSE from the fact that a user asked explicitly.
Every test becomes a read of a field, and cc->is_khugepaged goes, having
no reader left.

khugepaged fills the policy once per scan pass, MADV_COLLAPSE once per
call.  That is the one change in behaviour.  The max_ptes_* limits and the
defrag setting behind the allocation mask are sampled once per pass rather
than on every table.  A table scanned early in a pass and one scanned late
are then judged alike.

collapse_file() also drops a NULL check on the collapse_control.  It has
one call site, reached only from collapse_single_pmd(), which dereferences
cc unconditionally, so the check was already dead.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/* What a collapse is allowed to do, decided by the caller that asks for it */
+struct collapse_policy {
+	/* Limits, stated per PMD; HPAGE_PMD_NR means "no limit" */
+	unsigned int max_ptes_none;
+	unsigned int max_ptes_swap;
+	unsigned int max_ptes_shared;
+
+	/*
+	 * Hold a sub-PMD window to a stricter rule than a PMD: no swapped-out
+	 * and no shared PTEs at all, and max_ptes_none as
+	 * collapse_max_ptes_none() scales it.
+	 */
+	bool strict_sub_pmd;
+
+	/*
+	 * Collapse only where it looks worth doing: require some sign the
+	 * range is in use, and leave clean lazyfree folios for reclaim rather
+	 * than collapsing them into a folio that is not lazyfree.
+	 */
+	bool skip_lazyfree;
+	bool require_referenced;
+
+	/*
+	 * Finish the job rather than leaving it half done for a fault to pick
+	 * up: map the PMD over a file collapse before returning, and write
+	 * dirty pages back and retry once instead of refusing them.  Both cost
+	 * latency the caller has to be willing to pay.
+	 */
+	bool install_pmd;
+	bool writeback_dirty;
+
+	/* How hard to try for a destination folio */
+	gfp_t gfp;
+
+	/* Which VMAs are eligible, as thp_vma_allowable_orders() spells it */
+	enum tva_type tva_type;
+};

From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

collapse_control_init_scan() resets what one scan accumulates: the node
load, the allocation nodemask and the eligible-PTE bitmap.  It runs before
every PTE table a scan is given, not once per control, so its name points
at the wrong thing.

Call it collapse_scan_reset().

Preparation for giving a control a real init and release, run once each
for a whole series of scans.  Two names a word apart would then stand for
two different jobs.

No functional change.

2.54.0

---

From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

A collapse is two jobs.  One reads a PTE table under mmap_lock and decides
whether the range is worth collapsing.  The other allocates, isolates,
copies and flushes, and wants the lock given up first.

collapse_single_pmd() did both, so the boundary between them was somewhere
in the middle of a function.

Give each half its own function:

  - collapse_scan_pmd() scans one table and only reads.  The anonymous
    scan that used to carry that name keeps its body as
    collapse_scan_anon_pmd(), and collapse_scan_pmd() is now the entry
    that picks the anonymous or the file side.

  - collapse_run_pmd() does the collapse the scan asked for.
    SCAN_SUCCEED from the scan means there is something to run; anything
    else is why there is not.

collapse_single_pmd() is now the two of them with the mmap_lock drop in
between, so its callers see what they saw before.

Scan results (beyond SCAN_SUCCEED) communicated via collapse_control
structure: the orders, the referenced and swapped-out counts, and for a
file the file itself and the offset in it.

A file collapse works on the page cache and never sees a VMA.  The scan
takes the file reference while it still has VMA and the run unpins it
when it is done.

Tracing changes with it.  mm_khugepaged_scan_pmd now fires before
mm_collapse_huge_page instead of after it.  Its status field already reads
SCAN_SUCCEED for an accepted table, so what the collapse then made of that
table is mm_collapse_huge_page's to report, per order.

The two calls to that tracepoint become one.  They differed in what the
collapse between them changed; with the collapse no longer here, both
carry the same arguments.  failed_pfn is set only where a PTE was refused,
so it is -1 exactly when the result is SCAN_SUCCEED.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
---

index 05282eed9a35..f03cad8ed40e 100644

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a concern, explained reasoning
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed concerns about the locking contract in collapse_scan_pmd() and collapse_run_pmd(), agreeing to open-code the entry points in the callers khugepaged's walk and madvise_collapse(). The lock is given up and taken again at the same points as before, with no functional change. A fix is planned for v2.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

collapse_scan_pmd() and collapse_run_pmd() each have a clear locking
contract.  The scan is called with mmap_lock held for reading and returns
with it still held.  The collapse is called without it.

collapse_single_pmd() kept that boundary inside itself.  It dropped the
lock on some paths and not others, and reported which by way of a bool its
callers had to carry along and then act on.

Open-code it in the two callers.  Each scans under the lock it already
holds and, on SCAN_SUCCEED, gives the lock up before running the collapse.
khugepaged's lock_dropped and madvise_collapse()'s mmap_unlocked both go:
the code dropping the lock is now the code that wanted to know.

khugepaged's walk carries on to the next table while the scan keeps
refusing, and ends once a collapse has taken the lock from under it.
madvise_collapse() re-finds its VMA after a collapse, which it did before,
and now uses a NULL vma to say that it has to.  It still reports the drop
to its own caller, from the line that does it.

The lock is given up and taken again at the same points as before.  No
functional change.

Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

On Sat, Sep 05, 2026 at 05:23:42PM -0700, Andrew Morton wrote:
> > Behaviour
> > =========
> > 
> > Nothing here changes what gets collapsed.  Three things a reader should
> > not have to find in the diff:
> > 
> >  - Patch 5: khugepaged fills its policy once per scan pass, so the
> >    max_ptes_* limits and the defrag setting behind the allocation mask are
> >    sampled once per pass rather than once per table.  A table scanned early
> >    in a pass and one scanned late are then judged alike, where before a
> >    knob written mid-pass split them.
> > 
> >  - Patch 8: mm_khugepaged_scan_pmd fires before mm_collapse_huge_page
> >    rather than after it, the scan having returned before the collapse
> >    runs.  Its status field already reads SCAN_SUCCEED for an accepted
> >    table, so nothing changes there; what the collapse then made of the
> >    table is mm_collapse_huge_page's to report, per order.
> > 
> >  - Patch 10: which orders a table is scanned for is sampled once per VMA
> >    rather than once per table.  It cannot widen what a collapse does --
> >    the order is tested again under the lock the collapse retakes.
> 
> So is *any* functional change expected with this series?

No.

The three items here are so nobody has to find them in the diff, and
calling them behaviour changes is a stretch: a sysfs knob written
mid-pass takes effect on the next pass instead of the next table, and a
tracepoint fires a few lines earlier with the same arguments.


> I'm reluctant to merge an unreviewed v1, but this comes close!  I'll
> sit on it for a week or less, see what people say.  Please poke me
> if/when you think it's good for mm-new.

Zi and Baolin have already provided enough feedback to justify v2.

  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

On Fri, Sep 04, 2026 at 10:44:58PM -0400, Zi Yan wrote:
> On Fri Sep 4, 2026 at 11:10 AM EDT, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> > From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>
> >
> > Tests scattered through the collapse path decide what a collapse is
> > allowed to do by asking whether khugepaged started it.  Between them they
> > settle:
> >
> >  - which VMAs are eligible, and how hard to try for a folio;
> >  - how many empty, swapped-out or shared PTEs a window may contain, and
> >    whether a sub-PMD window is held to a stricter rule than a PMD;
> >  - whether a range has to look used, and whether a MADV_FREE'd page is
> >    left alone;
> >  - whether the PMD is mapped as part of the request, and whether dirty
> >    pages are worth writing back and retrying.
> >
> > None of those is a fact about khugepaged.  Each is something the caller
> > decided before asking, and the collapse code should not have to look up
> > who called to find out.
> >
> > Add struct collapse_policy for the caller to fill: khugepaged from its
> > own settings, MADV_COLLAPSE from the fact that a user asked explicitly.
> > Every test becomes a read of a field, and cc->is_khugepaged goes, having
> > no reader left.
> >
> > khugepaged fills the policy once per scan pass, MADV_COLLAPSE once per
> > call.  That is the one change in behaviour.  The max_ptes_* limits and the
> > defrag setting behind the allocation mask are sampled once per pass rather
> > than on every table.  A table scanned early in a pass and one scanned late
> > are then judged alike.
> >
> > collapse_file() also drops a NULL check on the collapse_control.  It has
> > one call site, reached only from collapse_single_pmd(), which dereferences
> > cc unconditionally, so the check was already dead.
> >
> > Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
> > Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
> > ---
> >  mm/collapse.h   |  40 ++++++++++++++++-
> >  mm/khugepaged.c | 114 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++----------------------
> >  2 files changed, 102 insertions(+), 52 deletions(-)
> >
> > diff --git a/mm/collapse.h b/mm/collapse.h
> > index 1c40229b9554..05282eed9a35 100644
> > --- a/mm/collapse.h
> > +++ b/mm/collapse.h
> > @@ -48,8 +48,46 @@ enum scan_result {
> >  	SCAN_PAGE_DIRTY_OR_WRITEBACK,
> >  };
> >  
> > +/* What a collapse is allowed to do, decided by the caller that asks for it */
> > +struct collapse_policy {
> > +	/* Limits, stated per PMD; HPAGE_PMD_NR means "no limit" */
> > +	unsigned int max_ptes_none;
> > +	unsigned int max_ptes_swap;
> > +	unsigned int max_ptes_shared;
> > +
> > +	/*
> > +	 * Hold a sub-PMD window to a stricter rule than a PMD: no swapped-out
> > +	 * and no shared PTEs at all, and max_ptes_none as
> > +	 * collapse_max_ptes_none() scales it.
> > +	 */
> > +	bool strict_sub_pmd;
> > +
> > +	/*
> > +	 * Collapse only where it looks worth doing: require some sign the
> > +	 * range is in use, and leave clean lazyfree folios for reclaim rather
> > +	 * than collapsing them into a folio that is not lazyfree.
> > +	 */
> > +	bool skip_lazyfree;
> > +	bool require_referenced;
> > +
> > +	/*
> > +	 * Finish the job rather than leaving it half done for a fault to pick
> > +	 * up: map the PMD over a file collapse before returning, and write
> > +	 * dirty pages back and retry once instead of refusing them.  Both cost
> > +	 * latency the caller has to be willing to pay.
> > +	 */
> > +	bool install_pmd;
> > +	bool writeback_dirty;
> > +
> > +	/* How hard to try for a destination folio */
> > +	gfp_t gfp;
> > +
> > +	/* Which VMAs are eligible, as thp_vma_allowable_orders() spells it */
> > +	enum tva_type tva_type;
> > +};
> > +
> >  struct collapse_control {
> > -	bool is_khugepaged;
> > +	struct collapse_policy policy;
> 
> Can it be made const since it seems to be read-only?

Not as a member: khugepaged fills it once per pass from the sysfs knobs,
so there is no constant object to point at. And madvise has to
initialize it somehow after cc is allocated.

It could become a const pointer to a struct the caller owns, but that
adds a lifetime to track for a struct that is written in one place and
read everywhere else.  I would rather keep it embedded.

-- 
  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

On Mon, Sep 07, 2026 at 05:05:19PM +0800, Baolin Wang wrote:
> > +/* What a collapse is allowed to do, decided by the caller that asks for it */

> > +
> > +	/*
> > +	 * Hold a sub-PMD window to a stricter rule than a PMD: no swapped-out
> > +	 * and no shared PTEs at all, and max_ptes_none as
> > +	 * collapse_max_ptes_none() scales it.
> > +	 */
> > +	bool strict_sub_pmd;
> 
> This is a bit confusing to me. Actually, the check for mTHP collapse is
> stricter.
> 
> How about naming it 'allow_mthp_collapse'? That way we can keep the most
> original comments for the collapse_max_ptes_xxx() functions, which is
> clearer to me.
> 
> If others have a better name, please ignore my comment.

I would rather keep strict_sub_pmd.

Which orders a caller asks for is already decided elsewhere:
collapse_possible_orders() hands khugepaged every anonymous order and
MADV_COLLAPSE the PMD order only, from tva_type.

A flag called allow_mthp_collapse next to that would read as
a second place deciding the same thing, and flipping it would not change
which orders get collapsed.  What it does change is how a sub-PMD window
is judged once one is asked for, and it is read at exactly the three
places that judge one.

I will do:

      /* Take no swapped-out or shared PTE into a sub-PMD collapse */
      bool strict_sub_pmd;

> 
> > +	/*
> > +	 * Collapse only where it looks worth doing: require some sign the
> > +	 * range is in use, and leave clean lazyfree folios for reclaim rather
> > +	 * than collapsing them into a folio that is not lazyfree.
> > +	 */
> > +	bool skip_lazyfree;
> > +	bool require_referenced;
> > +
> > +	/*
> > +	 * Finish the job rather than leaving it half done for a fault to pick
> > +	 * up: map the PMD over a file collapse before returning, and write
> > +	 * dirty pages back and retry once instead of refusing them.  Both cost
> > +	 * latency the caller has to be willing to pay.
> > +	 */
> 
> Can you simplify theses comments? I think the 'install_pmd' is easy to
> expalain. :)

Done for v2.

  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

On Sat, Sep 05, 2026 at 10:30:16PM -0400, Zi Yan wrote:
> On Fri Sep 4, 2026 at 11:10 AM EDT, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> > From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>
> >
> > A collapse is two jobs.  One reads a PTE table under mmap_lock and decides
> > whether the range is worth collapsing.  The other allocates, isolates,
> > copies and flushes, and wants the lock given up first.
> >
> > collapse_single_pmd() did both, so the boundary between them was somewhere
> > in the middle of a function.
> >
> > Give each half its own function:
> >
> >   - collapse_scan_pmd() scans one table and only reads.  The anonymous
> >     scan that used to carry that name keeps its body as
> >     collapse_scan_anon_pmd(), and collapse_scan_pmd() is now the entry
> >     that picks the anonymous or the file side.
> >
> >   - collapse_run_pmd() does the collapse the scan asked for.
> >     SCAN_SUCCEED from the scan means there is something to run; anything
> >     else is why there is not.
> >
> > collapse_single_pmd() is now the two of them with the mmap_lock drop in
> > between, so its callers see what they saw before.
> >
> > Scan results (beyond SCAN_SUCCEED) communicated via collapse_control
> > structure: the orders, the referenced and swapped-out counts, and for a
> > file the file itself and the offset in it.
> >
> > A file collapse works on the page cache and never sees a VMA.  The scan
> > takes the file reference while it still has VMA and the run unpins it
> > when it is done.
> >
> > Tracing changes with it.  mm_khugepaged_scan_pmd now fires before
> > mm_collapse_huge_page instead of after it.  Its status field already reads
> > SCAN_SUCCEED for an accepted table, so what the collapse then made of that
> > table is mm_collapse_huge_page's to report, per order.
> >
> > The two calls to that tracepoint become one.  They differed in what the
> > collapse between them changed; with the collapse no longer here, both
> > carry the same arguments.  failed_pfn is set only where a PTE was refused,
> > so it is -1 exactly when the result is SCAN_SUCCEED.

Signals: acknowledged a need to restructure, agreed to make changes
Zi Yan Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Zi Yan noted that khugepaged.c holds both the users of collapse and the machinery that performs it, creating a code organization issue where nothing marks where a user ends and the engine begins. He suggested adding mm/collapse.h to define what the two sides have to agree on, including enum scan_result and struct collapse_control.

On Fri Sep 4, 2026 at 11:10 AM EDT, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>
>
> madvise_collapse() holds an mmgrab() reference across its work. It is
> redundant. Every caller already holds mm_users:
>
>  - madvise(2) works on current->mm, which lives as long as the task is in
>    the syscall;
>  - process_madvise(2) reaches a remote mm through mm_access(), which takes
>    an mm_users reference and holds it until the syscall returns;
>  - io_uring passes current->mm;
>  - DAMON takes one with get_task_mm() and drops it after the call.
>
> Drop the mmgrab()/mmdrop() pair.

On Fri Sep 4, 2026 at 11:10 AM EDT, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

> khugepaged.c holds both the users of collapse and the machinery that
> performs it.  The daemon's scan loop, the sysfs tunables, MADV_COLLAPSE's

> begins.
>
> Start drawing that line.
>

>

On Fri Sep 4, 2026 at 11:10 AM EDT, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>
>
> Tests scattered through the collapse path decide what a collapse is
> allowed to do by asking whether khugepaged started it.  Between them they
> settle:
>
>  - which VMAs are eligible, and how hard to try for a folio;
>  - how many empty, swapped-out or shared PTEs a window may contain, and
>    whether a sub-PMD window is held to a stricter rule than a PMD;
>  - whether a range has to look used, and whether a MADV_FREE'd page is
>    left alone;
>  - whether the PMD is mapped as part of the request, and whether dirty
>    pages are worth writing back and retrying.
>
> None of those is a fact about khugepaged.  Each is something the caller
> decided before asking, and the collapse code should not have to look up
> who called to find out.
>
> Add struct collapse_policy for the caller to fill: khugepaged from its
> own settings, MADV_COLLAPSE from the fact that a user asked explicitly.
> Every test becomes a read of a field, and cc->is_khugepaged goes, having
> no reader left.
>
> khugepaged fills the policy once per scan pass, MADV_COLLAPSE once per
> call.  That is the one change in behaviour.  The max_ptes_* limits and the
> defrag setting behind the allocation mask are sampled once per pass rather
> than on every table.  A table scanned early in a pass and one scanned late
> are then judged alike.
>
> collapse_file() also drops a NULL check on the collapse_control.  It has
> one call site, reached only from collapse_single_pmd(), which dereferences
> cc unconditionally, so the check was already dead.
>
> Assisted-by: Claude-Code:claude-opus-5
> Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
> ---
>  mm/collapse.h   |  40 ++++++++++++++++-
>  mm/khugepaged.c | 114 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++----------------------
>  2 files changed, 102 insertions(+), 52 deletions(-)
>
> diff --git a/mm/collapse.h b/mm/collapse.h
> index 1c40229b9554..05282eed9a35 100644
> --- a/mm/collapse.h
> +++ b/mm/collapse.h
> @@ -48,8 +48,46 @@ enum scan_result {
>  	SCAN_PAGE_DIRTY_OR_WRITEBACK,
>  };
>  
> +/* What a collapse is allowed to do, decided by the caller that asks for it */
> +struct collapse_policy {
> +	/* Limits, stated per PMD; HPAGE_PMD_NR means "no limit" */
> +	unsigned int max_ptes_none;
> +	unsigned int max_ptes_swap;
> +	unsigned int max_ptes_shared;
> +
> +	/*
> +	 * Hold a sub-PMD window to a stricter rule than a PMD: no swapped-out
> +	 * and no shared PTEs at all, and max_ptes_none as
> +	 * collapse_max_ptes_none() scales it.
> +	 */
> +	bool strict_sub_pmd;
> +
> +	/*
> +	 * Collapse only where it looks worth doing: require some sign the
> +	 * range is in use, and leave clean lazyfree folios for reclaim rather
> +	 * than collapsing them into a folio that is not lazyfree.
> +	 */
> +	bool skip_lazyfree;
> +	bool require_referenced;
> +
> +	/*
> +	 * Finish the job rather than leaving it half done for a fault to pick
> +	 * up: map the PMD over a file collapse before returning, and write
> +	 * dirty pages back and retry once instead of refusing them.  Both cost
> +	 * latency the caller has to be willing to pay.
> +	 */
> +	bool install_pmd;
> +	bool writeback_dirty;
> +
> +	/* How hard to try for a destination folio */
> +	gfp_t gfp;
> +
> +	/* Which VMAs are eligible, as thp_vma_allowable_orders() spells it */
> +	enum tva_type tva_type;
> +};
> +
>  struct collapse_control {
> -	bool is_khugepaged;
> +	struct collapse_policy policy;

Can it be made const since it seems to be read-only?
